A company has deployed an AI-based solution to respond to dynamic queries related to its products and services. These queries evolve over time, and the solution must adapt quickly to new information. The company requires an efficient, scalable system that provides accurate responses in real-time while minimizing operational costs.

Which of the following options best satisfies the specified requirements?

- Continuously retrain the AI model using new data to ensure the model remains relevant and accurate.
- Store context data in a knowledge base and update it periodically as new information becomes available.
- **Retrieve relevant data using Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), and apply prompt engineering techniques.**
    
    **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)** is a method that enhances the output of large language models (LLMs) by referencing an authoritative knowledge base outside of their training data before generating a response. LLMs are trained on vast amounts of data and utilize billions of parameters to produce original outputs for various tasks, such as answering questions, translating languages, and completing sentences. RAG improves the already powerful capabilities of LLMs by allowing them to access specific domains or an organization’s internal knowledge base, without requiring any retraining of the model. This approach is cost-effective and ensures that LLM output remains relevant, accurate, and useful in different contexts.
    
    ![Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/rag-prompt-techniques-15JUL2025.png)
    
    The RAG model enhances user input (or prompts) by incorporating relevant retrieved data into the context. This process employs prompt engineering techniques to effectively communicate with the large language model (LLM). The augmented prompt enables the LLM to generate accurate responses to user queries.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Retrieve relevant data using Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), and apply prompt engineering techniques.** This solution enables the system to fetch up-to-date information at query time, ensuring responses remain accurate and contextually relevant without retraining the model. RAG separates the retrieval and generation processes, allowing scalable, cost-effective, and real-time adaptability. Prompt engineering further enhances response quality by guiding the model to focus on the most relevant aspects of the retrieved data.
    
    The option that says: **Continuously retrain the AI model using new data to ensure the model remains relevant and accurate** is incorrect because continuous retraining is primarily resource-intensive, costly, and time-consuming. It doesn’t scale well for real-time applications and introduces latency between data changes and model updates. It’s also inefficient for handling rapidly evolving queries.
    
    The option that says: **Implement a batch processing system that updates the model periodically with new data** is incorrect because batch updates introduce delays in reflecting new information. While more efficient than continuous retraining, it still lacks the agility needed for real-time responsiveness and may result in outdated answers between update cycles.
    
    The option that says: **Store context data in a knowledge base and update it periodically as new information becomes available** is incorrect because although maintaining a knowledge base is typically useful, periodic updates alone don’t guarantee real-time relevance. Without dynamic retrieval or integration with generative models, the system may fail to respond accurately to evolving queries or newly emerging topics.
    
- Implement a batch processing system that updates the model periodically with new data.

An organization uses an NLP application with a foundation model (FM). An ML Engineer evaluates its performance using the F1 score.

What does the F1 score specifically measure in this context?

- The F1 score evaluates the power consumption during model training.
- The F1 score evaluates the speed at which the model generates responses.
- The F1 score measures the cost-effectiveness of model inference during deployment.
- **The F1 score measures the model’s ability to balance precision and recall.**
    
    The **F1 score** is a metric used in classification problems to evaluate how well a model performs by considering both precision and recall. It is the harmonic mean of precision (the accuracy of positive predictions) and recall (the ability of the model to identify positive instances). A higher F1 score indicates a better balance between precision and recall, making it a useful metric for evaluating models when both false positives and false negatives are equally important.
    
    ![F1 Score](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-f1-score-17July2025.jpg)
    
    The F1 score is designed to handle situations where there is an imbalance between the classes. For instance, in NLP applications, it is essential to detect positive instances accurately (recall) while also ensuring that the predictions are precise (precision). This balance is crucial when the application involves tasks like text classification or sentiment analysis, where both false positives and false negatives can have significant consequences.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **The F1 score measures the model’s ability to balance precision and recall.**
    
    The option that says: **The F1 score evaluates the power consumption during model training** is incorrect because the F1 score is a performance metric related to the model’s prediction accuracy, not related to energy consumption during training. Power consumption is an operational concern, typically assessed with hardware-based metrics or specific tools, such as AWS Graviton processors, not through the F1 score.
    
    The option that says: **The F1 score evaluates the speed at which the model generates responses** is incorrect because speed simply refers to how quickly a model produces an output after receiving input, and is typically measured using inference time or response time metrics. The F1 score does not address speed or response time; it focuses on the accuracy of classification outcomes, specifically on balancing precision and recall.
    
    The option that says: **The F1 score measures the cost-effectiveness of model inference during deployment** is incorrect because cost-effectiveness refers to the efficiency and resource consumption of deploying a model, not its classification accuracy. The F1 score is a metric to evaluate the performance of the model’s predictions, whereas cost-effectiveness would be measured using other metrics related to operational costs, like cost per inference.
    

A company is developing a machine learning application with Amazon Bedrock. It requires consistent throughput and a fixed capacity for reliable performance and cost efficiency.

Which pricing model should the company choose?

- Batch
- Latency optimized
- Model customization
- **Provisioned Throughput**
    
    The **Provisioned Throughput** pricing model in Amazon Bedrock is tailored for applications that require consistent and predictable performance. With this model, customers commit to a fixed level of throughput, ensuring that their application can handle a steady volume of requests without interruptions. This approach is ideal for workloads that need guaranteed, low-latency, and high-throughput processing, such as real-time applications in machine learning, which need to scale without fluctuating costs. By committing to provisioned throughput, customers can plan their costs more effectively and avoid sudden spikes in charges.
    
    ![Provisioned Throughput](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-provisioned-throughput-17July2025.png)
    
    For this company, Provisioned Throughput is the best choice because it provides reliable and uninterrupted service with consistent capacity. The model offers cost savings and consistent performance, unlike on-demand pricing or batch processing.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Provisioned Throughput.**
    
    **Model customization** is incorrect because it primarily focuses on customizing models and charges based on training tokens and model storage, with inference costs under the Provisioned Throughput plan. This option is not suited for the company’s need for consistent throughput, as it requires model training and a commitment to additional units for inference.
    
    **Latency optimized** is incorrect because it focuses on reducing response times for specific models, like Amazon Nova Pro and Claude 3.5 Haiku, which improves performance for generative AI applications. However, it doesn’t guarantee the consistent throughput needed for steady, high-volume requests like the one required in this scenario.
    
    **Batch** is incorrect because it is typically designed for processing large volumes of requests in bulk, providing predictions in a single output file. This is ideal for non-time-sensitive tasks, but it doesn’t offer the real-time performance and consistent throughput required for the company’s application.
    

A company is training an AI model that processes large datasets in batch jobs. The workload is fault-tolerant and can tolerate interruptions. To minimize costs, the company wants to run these jobs during off-peak hours when cloud resource demand is lower.

Which AWS pricing model is the most cost-effective for this scenario?

- On-Demand Instance
- **Spot Instance**
    
    **Amazon EC2 Spot Instances** allow you to purchase unused EC2 capacity at significantly reduced prices up to 90% lower than On-Demand rates. This makes them an ideal solution for running cost-sensitive, flexible workloads such as AI/ML model training, big data analytics, and other batch-processing tasks. Since Spot Instances are interruptible, they are best suited for workloads that can tolerate pauses or interruptions, especially those that can be checkpointed and resumed later.
    
    ![Amazon EC2-Spot Instances](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Amazon-EC2-Spot-Instances-07-16-2025.png)
    
    A primary consideration when using Spot Instances is their potential for interruption—AWS can reclaim these instances with only a two-minute notice when the capacity is required for other workloads. Despite this, the impact is minimal for many AI use cases, especially batch jobs scheduled during off-peak hours. These types of workloads are typically designed to be fault-tolerant, and AWS services like AWS Batch, EC2 Auto Scaling, and Amazon SageMaker AI help mitigate interruptions by automatically distributing tasks and restarting jobs as needed.
    
    By designing workloads to handle potential interruptions and leveraging Spot Instances for the right use cases, organizations can dramatically lower their compute costs without sacrificing scalability or performance. For companies training AI models that don’t require continuous availability, Spot Instances are the most cost-effective pricing model, offering a balance of flexibility, scalability, and savings.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Spot Instance.**
    
    **Reserved Capacity** is incorrect because it is typically used for predictable, long-term workloads that require continuous compute resources. While it offers cost savings over On-Demand pricing, it is only cost-effective when you commit to using the instance over a one- or three-year term, regardless of actual usage. Since the company in the scenario plans to run jobs only during off-peak hours and has a flexible, fault-tolerant workload, paying for constant reserved capacity would result in unnecessary costs.
    
    **Savings Plan** is incorrect. This pricing model offers cost saving across a range of compute services but only work well when compute usage is consistent over time. They are typically chosen for always-on or regularly scheduled workloads. Since the company’s batch jobs are flexible, interruption-tolerant, and expected to run sporadically, committing to a fixed hourly spend just wouldn’t align with the nature of their workload and may result in underutilized commitments.
    
    **On-Demand Instance** is incorrect. While it offers maximum flexibility, you just pay for compute as you use it, with no long-term commitment, this comes at the highest cost per hour. For fault-tolerant batch jobs that run during off-peak hours and can tolerate interruptions, using On-Demand pricing is simply not cost-efficient. The company can typically achieve the same compute capacity and performance at a much lower cost by using Spot Instances, which are specifically designed for this type of workload.
    
- Reserved Capacity
- Savings Plan

A large retail bank is building an AI-powered virtual assistant to streamline the customer experience around reporting and disputing fraudulent transactions. The assistant must be able to retrieve account and transaction details, submit fraud claims through secure internal APIs, and guide customers through the process using clear, natural language explanations. At the same time, it must strictly enforce data privacy policies and ensure no sensitive information is exposed in responses.

Which AWS solution best supports this use case by combining large language models (LLMs) with secure backend integration?

- Amazon Comprehend
- **Agents for Amazon Bedrock**
    
    **Agents for Amazon Bedrock** allow developers to build AI-powered applications that combine the reasoning abilities of foundation models with the ability to take real-world actions like retrieving data or calling APIs securely and reliably. These agents extend the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by enabling them to follow instructions, use external tools, and manage multi-step tasks using a pre-defined set of “actions.” Each action is mapped to an API call or function that the agent can invoke based on user input, making it possible to build intelligent assistants that interact with enterprise systems.
    
    ![Agents for Amazon Bedrock](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Agents-for-Amazon-Bedrock-07-16-2025.png)
    
    This service is particularly useful in customer-facing scenarios where natural conversation must be paired with secure and structured backend integration. For instance, in a banking context, an agent can securely retrieve account details, file fraud claims, and provide status updates—all while maintaining strict adherence to privacy and access controls. Developers define how the agent interacts with APIs and what it is allowed to access, giving full control over the integration points, user permissions, and response formatting. The agents can also chain together multiple steps, allowing them to manage complex workflows with little to no manual orchestration.
    
    Because Agents for Amazon Bedrock are built within the managed Amazon Bedrock service, they benefit from the scalability, security, and compliance of the AWS ecosystem. They support multiple foundation models from leading providers like Anthropic (Claude), Meta (Llama), and Amazon Titan, enabling organizations to choose the best model for their use case without having to manage model infrastructure. These capabilities make Bedrock Agents a powerful solution for enterprises that need secure, conversational AI tools that can safely interact with internal data and services.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Agents for Amazon Bedrock.**
    
    **Amazon Comprehend** is incorrect because it is a natural language processing (NLP) service that is primarily used to extract insights such as sentiment, key phrases, and named entities from text. It can help analyze written communication, but it only processes unstructured text; it does not support actions, API integrations, or interactive conversation flows. It simply lacks the ability to orchestrate workflows or access backend systems, which is critical for securely submitting fraud claims or retrieving account information.
    
    **Amazon Q** is incorrect. This service is a generative AI assistant primarily designed to help business users and developers interact with data, applications, and documentation. While it does use LLMs, it is just tailored for internal productivity use cases like code suggestions, document summarization, and knowledge retrieval within organizations. It does not support secure API calls or backend integrations in customer-facing workflows, so it would not be suitable for guiding users through fraud claims in real time.
    
    **Amazon Polly** is incorrect because it is a text-to-speech (TTS) service that is only used to convert written text into lifelike speech. While it may be used as part of a voice assistant to enhance accessibility or user experience, it simply does not provide conversational intelligence, LLM capabilities, or any kind of backend API access.
    
- Amazon Q
- Amazon Polly

A customer support team at a tech company developed a large language model (LLM) to assist with frequently asked questions and troubleshooting. After fine-tuning the model with recent data, the team uses Amazon Comprehend for various natural language understanding tasks. To verify whether the fine-tuning has improved the model’s accuracy, the team needs to evaluate its performance.

Which evaluation metric would be most appropriate to measure the model’s effectiveness?

- **F1 Score**
    
    The **F1 Score** is a commonly used metric for evaluating classification models, especially when dealing with imbalanced datasets. It combines both precision and recall into a single value, measuring the model’s ability to correctly identify positive cases while minimizing false positives and false negatives. This balance is crucial in many real-world applications where both types of errors carry weight. When fine-tuning large language models (LLMs), the F1 Score becomes especially important, as it provides a clear indication of whether the model’s predictive performance has genuinely improved across both precision and recall.
    
    ![F1 Score Confusion Matrix](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-F1ScoreConfusionMatrix-16July2025.png)
    
    By taking into account both precision and recall, the F1 Score offers a more reliable and well-rounded assessment of model effectiveness than using either metric alone. This is particularly valuable in natural language tasks supported by LLMs, where the cost of missing relevant responses or incorrectly flagging irrelevant ones can be high. Using the F1 Score helps ensure that fine-tuning efforts lead to meaningful and balanced improvements in the model’s real-world outputs.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **F1 Score.**
    
    **Area Under the ROC Curve** is incorrect because it is primarily used for binary classification tasks and measures the model’s ability to distinguish between two classes. While the ROC curve can be useful for evaluating models in terms of their true positive rate and false positive rate, it is not as well-suited for tasks involving the fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) that require a balanced evaluation of both false positives and false negatives, which the F1 Score captures more effectively.
    
    **Precision** is incorrect because it focuses only on the proportion of true positive predictions relative to the total number of predicted positives. While precision is valuable for certain types of evaluation, it doesn’t consider the model’s ability to identify all relevant instances (recall). In tasks where both false positives and false negatives are important, precision alone typically provides an incomplete view of model performance, making the F1 Score a more comprehensive choice for evaluating fine-tuned models.
    
    **Mean Squared Error** is incorrect because it is typically used for regression tasks, where the output is continuous and measures the squared differences between predicted and actual values. This metric is not suitable for evaluating classification models, where the goal is to categorize inputs into discrete classes. For classification tasks, metrics like F1 Score provide a better understanding of how well the model distinguishes between different categories.
    
- Precision
- Mean Squared Error
- Area Under the ROC Curve

A retail company wants to integrate sentiment analysis into its customer feedback system using a large language model (LLM) on Amazon Bedrock. The company aims to analyze the emotional tone of customer reviews stored in Amazon S3 and generate consistent responses to identical input prompts. To optimize the analysis and improve results, the company will fine-tune specific inference parameters for the LLM.

To make sure the LLM generates uniform responses for identical prompts, what modification should the company make to the inference parameter?

- Decrease the Top-P parameter
- **Lower the temperature value**
    
    **Amazon Bedrock** is a fully managed service from AWS that gives users access to a variety of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) suited for different AI applications. It helps simplify the integration of machine learning models into applications by taking care of infrastructure and model maintenance. One important feature of Amazon Bedrock is the ability to adjust **inference parameters**, which influence how the model generates responses. Parameters such as temperature, top-p, and top-k can be fine-tuned to control how random, diverse, or consistent the outputs are. These adjustments allow companies to customize model behavior according to their specific needs.
    
    ![Temperature inference parameter](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/temperature-inference-parameter-16JUL2025.png)
    
    The **temperature** parameter is especially important in controlling how varied a model’s responses can be. A high temperature results in more diverse and less predictable outputs, while a low temperature leads to more focused and consistent answers. When consistency is a priority, such as when analyzing large amounts of customer feedback, predictable and repeatable responses are essential. Lowering the temperature helps ensure that the model gives similar outputs for the same input across different sessions.
    
    By adjusting the temperature downward, the model becomes more consistent and stable in its replies. This is particularly useful when performing repeated analysis of similar input prompts. It helps maintain reliable performance over time, which is critical in use cases like sentiment analysis. A lower temperature guides the model toward more likely and expected responses, making its behavior easier to trust and reproduce.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Lower the temperature value.**
    
    The option that says: **Increase the Top-K parameter** is incorrect because increasing the top-k parameter allows the model to sample from a larger set of potential next tokens, which can introduce more variability and randomness in the model’s output. This goes against the need for consistency in generating uniform responses to identical inputs, as increasing top-k typically results in more diverse outputs.
    
    The option that says: **Increase the temperature value** is incorrect because increasing the temperature makes the model’s responses more random and less predictable. A higher temperature encourages the model to explore more diverse outputs, which is useful for creative tasks but not ideal for scenarios where consistent and stable responses are needed. Increasing the temperature simply introduces more variability, which would reduce the uniformity of the model’s responses.
    
    The option that says: **Decrease the Top-P parameter** is incorrect because decreasing the top-p parameter would limit the diversity of the model’s token selection, but it doesn’t directly address the need for consistency in responses. Lowering the top-p setting primarily restricts the model to a narrower range of possible token options, which might reduce randomness, but it does not directly influence the predictability or uniformity of responses.
    
- Increase the temperature value
- Increase the Top-K parameter

A financial services company stores customer feedback forms in Amazon S3. These forms are submitted through a web portal hosted on Amazon API Gateway and often contain sensitive data such as Social Security numbers and credit card details. The goal is to automatically detect and alert on the presence of sensitive data in the uploaded forms with minimal development effort.

Which of the following will meet the given requirements?

- Deploy a large language model (LLM) on an Amazon SageMaker endpoint to analyze uploaded forms for sensitive data and generate alerts.
- Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the text for sensitive data and trigger alerts via an AWS Lambda function.
- **Use Amazon Macie to monitor the S3 bucket for sensitive data and deliver alerts when such data is detected.**
    
    Amazon Macie is a data security service that uses machine learning and pattern matching to identify sensitive data. It offers visibility into data security risks and enables automated protection against these risks.
    
    To help you manage the security of your organization’s Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) data, Macie provides an inventory of your general-purpose S3 buckets and automatically evaluates and monitors these buckets for security and access control. If Macie detects a potential issue with the security or privacy of your data, such as a bucket becoming publicly accessible, it generates a finding for you to review and remediate as necessary.
    
    Macie automates the discovery and reporting of sensitive data, helping you gain a better understanding of the data your organization stores in Amazon S3. To identify sensitive data, you can use the built-in criteria and techniques provided by Macie, create your own custom criteria, or combine both options. If Macie detects sensitive data in an S3 object, it generates a finding to notify you about the sensitive data it has identified.
    
    ![Amazon Macie](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-macie-16JUL2025.png)
    
    Macie not only presents findings but also offers statistics and insights into the security posture of your Amazon S3 data, highlighting where sensitive information may be located within your data estate. This data can help you decide which specific S3 buckets and objects warrant deeper investigation. You can review and analyze findings, statistics, and additional information using either the Amazon Macie console or the Amazon Macie API.
    
    Additionally, you can utilize Macie’s integration with Amazon EventBridge and AWS Security Hub to monitor, process, and address findings through other services, applications, and systems.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon Macie to monitor the S3 bucket for sensitive data and deliver alerts when such data is detected.**
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Comprehend to analyze the text for sensitive data and trigger alerts via an AWS Lambda function** is incorrect because Amazon Comprehend is primarily used for natural language processing tasks such as sentiment analysis and entity recognition. While it can identify some types of entities, it does not natively support sensitive data detection like PII or financial information. Additionally, integrating it with Lambda for alerting adds development complexity, which violates the requirement for minimal effort.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Glue to create a custom ETL job that detects and redacts sensitive data in uploaded forms before storing them in S3** is incorrect because AWS Glue can typically detect and redact sensitive data using its Detect PII transform, but it requires building and maintaining ETL jobs. This involves schema configuration, transformation logic, and scheduling, which introduces moderate to high development effort. It’s better suited for batch processing rather than real-time detection and alerting.
    
    The option that says: **Deploy a large language model (LLM) on an Amazon SageMaker endpoint to analyze uploaded forms for sensitive data and generate alerts** is incorrect because using SageMaker to deploy an LLM for sensitive data detection involves significant development work, including model selection, training or fine-tuning, endpoint management, and custom alerting logic. This approach is powerful but far exceeds the requirement for minimal development effort and lacks the out-of-the-box capabilities that Macie provides.
    
- Use AWS Glue to create a custom ETL job that detects and redacts sensitive data in uploaded forms before storing them in S3.

A research team is using a large language model (LLM) for a customer support application. However, the model is frequently generating hallucinations, providing incorrect or fabricated responses.

Which action would help reduce hallucinations in the model’s output?

- **Lower the temperature inference parameter to produce more focused and deterministic outputs.**
    
    Hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) refer to instances where the model generates responses that sound plausible but are factually incorrect or entirely fabricated. These errors often occur when the model lacks sufficient grounding in reliable data or attempts to fill informational gaps by making confident yet unfounded inferences. Hallucinations stem from the fundamental nature of language modeling, which prioritizes fluency and contextual relevance over factual precision. As a result, even well-structured outputs may mislead users if not carefully validated against trusted sources.
    
    ![temperature inference parameter](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/temperature-inference-parameter-16JUL2025.png)
    
    Temperature influences the randomness of a model’s output. A lower temperature setting (e.g., between 0.2 and 0.5) leads to more deterministic responses, making the model less likely to produce creative but inaccurate answers. By decreasing the temperature, the randomness in the model’s responses is reduced. This results in output that is more stable and less likely to contain errors, which is particularly important for customer support, where factual accuracy is essential.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Lower the temperature inference parameter to produce more focused and deterministic outputs.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement Agents for Amazon Bedrock to oversee and correct inference outputs** is incorrect. Although agents can typically be used to manage outputs, they do not directly address the issue of hallucinations.
    
    The option that says: **Fine-tune the model with domain-specific data to improve accuracy** is incorrect. Although fine-tuning can improve domain relevance, it does not directly prevent hallucinations. If the fine-tuning data is not carefully curated, it can introduce new inaccuracies. Additionally, fine-tuning is resource-intensive and not always feasible for every use case.
    
    The option that says: **Use data augmentation techniques to add more diverse input data for training** is incorrect because data augmentation primarily increases training data diversity, which can help generalization, but does not specifically target hallucination reduction.
    
- Use data augmentation techniques to add more diverse input data for training.
- Implement Agents for Amazon Bedrock to oversee and correct inference outputs.
- Fine-tune the model with domain-specific data to improve accuracy.

A research institute is using a foundation model (FM) for real-time data analysis and needs a training strategy that regularly updates the model with new data to maintain its relevance and accuracy.

Which of the following will meet the requirements?

- Fine-tuning
- **Continuous pre-training**
    
    Continued pre-training (or continuous pre-training) is the process of further training a pre-trained foundation model on new data using the same unsupervised objectives, such as masked language modeling or causal language modeling. This method retains the model’s previously acquired general language capabilities while enabling it to adapt to new domains or changes in data distribution.
    
    CPT does not involve any architectural modifications or fine-tuning for specific downstream tasks. Instead, it enhances the model’s language understanding capacity in a way that is aware of the new domain.
    
    ![Continued pre-training (CPT)](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/continued-pre-training-17JUL2025.png)
    
    Continued Pre-training: This process involves providing unlabeled data to pre-train a foundation model, allowing it to become familiar with specific types of inputs. You can supply data from particular topics to help the model gain exposure to those areas. Continued pre-training adjusts the model’s parameters to better accommodate this input data and enhance its domain knowledge.
    
    For instance, you can train a model using private data, such as business documents, that are not publicly accessible for training large language models. Furthermore, you can continuously improve the model by retraining it with additional unlabeled data as it becomes available.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Continuous pre-training.**
    
    **Static training** is incorrect because it primarily involves training the model once on a fixed dataset without any updates. This strategy fails to incorporate new data, making the model less effective over time as the data distribution changes. It’s unsuitable for real-time scenarios where freshness and adaptability are crucial.
    
    **Transfer learning** is incorrect because it focuses on adapting a pre-trained model to a new task or domain using a smaller, task-specific dataset. While useful for domain adaptation, it doesn’t inherently support continuous updates or real-time data integration, which are essential for maintaining relevance in evolving environments.
    
    **Fine-tuning** is incorrect because it typically involves adjusting a pre-trained model using a specific dataset to improve performance on a particular task. Although it can improve accuracy, it’s generally a one-time or infrequent process and doesn’t meet the requirement for regular updates with new data unless paired with a continuous learning framework.
    
- Static training
- Transfer learning

A company uses Amazon Lex and Amazon Bedrock to deploy a customer support chatbot that handles sensitive queries like account access and payments. To prevent prompt injection attacks, the team needs a secure prompting technique that can detect and mitigate malicious inputs.

Which prompting technique best protects the chatbot from prompt injection while ensuring accurate responses?

- Maieutic prompting
- Zero-shot prompting
- Few-shot prompting
- **Adversarial prompting**
    
    **Adversarial prompting** is a technique used to test the robustness and safety of language models by deliberately crafting inputs designed to exploit weaknesses in the model’s behavior. These inputs, also known as prompt injection attacks, attempt to override the model’s original instructions, elicit harmful or unauthorized outputs, or expose sensitive system behaviors. By simulating malicious or manipulative user input, adversarial prompting allows developers to identify and address vulnerabilities before models are deployed in production, especially in environments where data privacy, trust, and security are critical, such as banking or healthcare.
    
    ![Adversarial Prompting](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Adversarial-Prompting-07-17-2025.png)
    
    In AWS services like Amazon Bedrock, adversarial prompting plays an important role in evaluating and hardening LLM-based applications, such as customer support chatbots. These models are often integrated with internal tools, APIs, or sensitive data, making them potential targets for injection attacks or misuse. Running adversarial prompt tests helps ensure that LLMs respond appropriately to suspicious or malformed input, and it supports the implementation of defensive techniques such as input validation, output filtering, and context separation.
    
    By using adversarial prompting during development and testing, teams can enforce secure prompt design, apply the principle of least privilege, and create safer AI applications that align with AWS’s guidance for responsible AI. This practice is essential in any system that relies on generative AI for decision-making, automation, or access to private data.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Adversarial prompting.**
    
    **Few-shot prompting** is incorrect because it involves giving a language model just a few example inputs and outputs within the prompt to guide its responses. While this technique can improve response consistency and relevance, it is only useful for shaping behavior, not for preventing security threats like prompt injection. It simply doesn’t include any mechanism for detecting or blocking malicious user input designed to override instructions.
    
    **Zero-shot prompting** is incorrect because it gives the model only a task description without examples, relying on its general understanding. While this approach can be efficient, it is typically more open-ended and unpredictable, making it even more vulnerable to prompt injection attacks. Without structured examples or protective logic, zero-shot prompts offer little defense against malicious manipulation.
    
    **Maieutic prompting** is incorrect because it is an advanced reasoning technique used primarily to validate the internal consistency or truthfulness of an LLM’s response by recursively questioning it. While useful in evaluating model reasoning, it just isn’t designed to handle security threats like prompt injection. It’s a verification tool and simply lacks the safeguards needed to protect sensitive systems.
    

A financial company has built a binary classification model using Amazon SageMaker AI to detect fraudulent transactions. The model outputs a probability between 0 and 1 for each prediction. Since fraud cases are rare, the team needs to evaluate the model’s performance while accounting for class imbalance.

Which metric best measures the model’s ability to distinguish between fraud and non-fraud across all thresholds?

- **Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC) Score**
    
    The **Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUC-ROC)** is a widely used evaluation metric for binary classification models, especially in scenarios where classes are imbalanced, such as fraud detection. The ROC curve plots the True Positive Rate (TPR) against the False Positive Rate (FPR) at various classification thresholds. The AUC score summarizes this curve as a single number between 0 and 1, representing the model’s overall ability to distinguish between the positive and negative classes. A model with an AUC of 1.0 has perfect discrimination, while an AUC of 0.5 means the model performs no better than random guessing.
    
    ![Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUC-ROC)](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/TD-Area-Under-the-Receiver-Operating-Characteristic-Curve-07-17-2025.png)
    
    A key advantage of AUC is that it is threshold-independent. This means it evaluates the model’s ranking performance rather than its predictions at any one fixed threshold. For fraud detection systems, where the positive class (fraud) is rare, accuracy or threshold-based metrics like precision may give misleading results. AUC provides a holistic view of the model’s performance, helping data scientists select the best threshold for real-world deployment or compare different models more objectively.
    
    In Amazon SageMaker AI, AUC is commonly used during model evaluation as part of training reports and model tuning processes. It helps teams understand how well a model separates legitimate from fraudulent transactions, and it’s especially useful when paired with confusion matrix metrics and cost-sensitive analysis.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC) Score.**
    
    **Accuracy** is incorrect because it is typically not reliable for imbalanced datasets like fraud detection, where the majority of transactions are non-fraudulent. A model that predicts “legitimate” for every transaction could still achieve high accuracy, but would completely fail to detect actual fraud cases. It only reflects overall correctness, without showing how well the model separates the minority class.
    
    **F1-Score** is incorrect because it is primarily useful when false positives and false negatives carry similar costs. However, it still reflects performance at a specific threshold only, and simply doesn’t provide a full picture of how the model ranks predictions. AUC is preferred when comparing models or evaluating general discrimination ability over all thresholds.
    
    **Precision** is incorrect. This metric measures the percentage of predicted fraud cases that are actually fraud, which is useful but only tells part of the story. It focuses just on positive predictions, ignoring how many real fraud cases were missed. Since it’s threshold-dependent, it does not account for overall model performance across all possible thresholds, making it less helpful for early model evaluation.
    
- Precision
- F1-Score
- Accuracy

Unlike traditional keyword-based search, Amazon Kendra relies on semantic and contextual similarity, along with sophisticated ranking capabilities, to determine the relevance of text chunks or documents in response to a query.

You can leverage your document metadata to build a feature-rich and customized search experience for your users, helping them efficiently find the right answers to their questions.

    **Amazon Q** is incorrect because it is an AI-powered assistant focused on improving productivity, answering questions, and generating code or documentation. A company is building an interactive customer support solution that needs to convert text-based responses into natural-sounding speech and allow users to interact using voice.

Which AWS service should the company use to implement text-to-speech functionality?

- Amazon SageMaker AI
- Amazon Transcribe
- Amazon Lex
- **Amazon Polly**
    
    **Amazon Polly** is an advanced cloud-based text-to-speech (TTS) service offered by AWS that converts written text into lifelike, human-sounding speech. It supports over 100 voices in more than 40 languages and variants, including neural and generative voice engines that produce high-quality, emotionally expressive speech. Polly allows developers to customize speech output using Speech Synthesis Markup Language (SSML), enabling control over aspects like pronunciation, pitch, rate, volume, and emphasis. The service is designed for low-latency applications, making it suitable for real-time interactions such as voice assistants and customer support systems. Polly also supports various audio formats like MP3, OGG, and PCM, and offers features like custom lexicons for pronunciation adjustments and the ability to cache speech outputs for cost efficiency.
    
    ![Amazon Polly](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-amazon-polly-17July2025.png)
    
    In the given scenario, where a company is developing an interactive customer support solution that requires converting text-based responses into natural-sounding speech and enabling voice interactions, Amazon Polly is the most appropriate AWS service. Polly’s capabilities align perfectly with the need for high-quality, real-time speech synthesis. Its support for multiple languages and voices allows for a personalized customer experience, while features like SSML enable fine-tuned control over speech characteristics, ensuring clarity and engagement. Additionally, Polly’s integration with other AWS services, such as Amazon Connect, facilitates the creation of seamless voice-enabled applications, making it an ideal choice for building scalable and interactive customer support solutions.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon Polly.**
    
    **Amazon Lex** is incorrect. While it is typically used for building conversational interfaces and voice-enabled chatbots, it does not specialize in converting text into speech. Lex primarily focuses on understanding and processing voice input from users to generate text-based responses or trigger actions, but it does not synthesize speech.
    
    **Amazon Transcribe** is incorrect because this service is a speech-to-text service, meaning it converts spoken language into written text. While this is useful for transcribing customer calls or conversations, it does not provide text-to-speech functionality.
    
    **Amazon SageMaker AI** is incorrect because this is a machine learning service primarily designed for building, training, and deploying models at scale. While SageMaker excels at tasks such as model development, it is not intended for text-to-speech conversion. The primary use case of SageMaker is for custom machine learning models, not for converting text into speech.
    

A data science team needs a user-friendly interface for quick experimentation with Amazon Bedrock foundation models in a non-production environment.

PartyRock provides a user-friendly interface that allows for quick experimentation with various foundation models, making it an ideal platform for rapid prototyping and learning.
